1.4.2. Estimation d’indices d’abondance et modèle de production 
de biomasse 
o Modélisation d’indice d’abondance 
Le calcul d'indices d'abondance est une étape clés de l'évaluation des stocks. De tels 
indices présentent un intérêt intrinsèque car ils renseignent sur les tendances 
d'évolution de la ressource. Ils  sont également à la base de la plupart des modèles 
de dynamique des populations, notamment pour le calcul d'efforts de pêche 
théoriques et pour l'ajustement des modèles de production, ou pour la calibration des 
méthodes d'analyse des cohortes. Ces indices sont généralement estimés à partir 
des données d'observations issues des campagnes scientifiques, ou à partir des 
statistiques de pêches issues des flottilles commerciales. Dans un cas comme dans 
l'autre, estimer des indices d'abondance reste cependant une tâche relativement 
complexe, compte tenu notamment de plan d'échantillonnage souvent fortement 
déséquilibrés. 
Le modèle linéaire généralisé (GLM) est aujourd’hui la méthode la plus courante pour 
estimer les indices d’abondance. Le GLM est une extension du Modèle linéaire (LM) 
dont l’utilisation est limitée à certaines conditions, notamment la normalité des 
résidus ce qui n’est souvent pas le cas dans les données halieutiques. Le principe du 
modèle est de faire le lien entre des variables à expliquer (réponses) et des variables 
explicatives. Le modèle ajusté aux données est une représentation mathématique de 
la relation entre ces variables, et permet de mieux comprendre dans la réalité le 
phénomène qui a généré ces données.  
Le choix des variables explicatives à conserver dans le modèle statistique doit être 
fait en testant plusieurs modèles correspondant à plusieurs hypothèses. La 
comparaison permet alors de définir les variables explicatives à retenir (Laurans, 
2005). Dans le présent travail l’utilisation d’un GLM a pour objectif d’estimer un indice 
d’abondance standardisé par espèce, à partir des CPUE de la flotte nationale 
Mauritanienne, et/ou à partir des indices d’abondances issus des campagnes 
scientifiques. 
La modélisation des CPUE permet de prendre en compte les variations spatio-
temporelles de la disponibilité de la ressource et les différences de puissance de 


58 
 
pêche des catégories de navires qui peuvent avoir des effets significatifs sur la 
CPUE (à savoir : la puissance motrice du navire, son type de conservation et sa 
licence de pêche). Les effets de plusieurs facteurs sont donc testés : effet an, effet 
saison, effet mois, effet TJB, effet type de conservation (glacier ou congélateur) et 
effet licence de pêche. Certain facteurs sont testés avec différentes stratifications.  
La modélisation des indices d’abondance (IA) des campagnes scientifiques permet 
aussi de prendre en compte les variations spatio-temporelles de la ressource. Dans 
ce cas, les enregistrements nuls pour certaines espèces peu occurrentes biaisent 
souvent la normalité de la distribution des IA. Pour remédier à cela on recourt à deux 
sous modèles, l’un pour modéliser l’absence et la présence de l’espèce et l’autre 
pour modéliser les valeurs positives des IA. La combinaison des deux sous-modèles 
conduit au modèle Delta (Stefansson 1996 ; Le Pape et al., 2003).   
Plusieurs sources de biais peuvent être citées concernant l’estimation de ces indices. 
La première source de biais est la non proportionnalité entre les CPUE et 
l’abondance réelle.  Il est notamment difficile d’intégrer dans le temps l’évolution des 
puissances de pêche des navires liée soit à des changements de répartition spatiale 
de leur effort de pêche, soit à des processus d’innovations technologiques. En outre, 
le changement des rendements au cours des années peut être lié au changement 
des stratégies de pêche des bateaux commerciaux suite aux contraintes de marchés 
ou bien à la raréfaction d’une espèce cible comme le poulpe chez les 
Céphalopodiers nationaux mauritaniens. Cependant l’avantage des CPUE est la 
faible variance statistique des estimateurs obtenus du fait du grand nombre 
d’opération de pêche prises en compte 
Le principal reproche qui peut être fait aux indices de campagnes scientifiques est à 
l’inverse le faible taux d’échantillonnage, notamment le faible nombre de traits 
effectués par strate (bathymétrique ou zone) suite au coût très élevé de ces 
campagnes. A titre d’exemple un jour de mer coûte en moyenne 1,5 millions 
ouguiyas (4 mille euros environ) à l’IMROP, avec une  réalisation de 5 à 10 traits de 
pêche par jour d’une durée de 30 minutes chacun. La campagne dure une quinzaine 
de jours au minimum, et couvre une surface de l’ordre de 5 km², alors que le plateau 
continental est d’une superficie de 33 mille km² environ.   
 


59 
 
1.4.3. Modèles de production de biomasse 
 
o Principes du modèle 
Le Modèle de Production de Biomasse, ou « modèle global », est un outil classique 
d’évaluation des stocks. Il est l’un des modèles les plus utilisé pour les évaluations 
de stock lorsque la quantité de données est limitée (données de capture en poids et 
indice d’abondance) (Hilborn et Walters 1992). Il s’appui sur une hypothèse 
fondamentale d’équilibre : une population (ou un stock) à l’état vierge tend vers une 
situation d’équilibre  stable2 avec son milieu. Le modèle global, s’il est moins détaillé 
que les modèles de déplétion ou de VPA offre des avantages :  
- 
il est parcimonieux (peu de paramètres) et peu exigeant en données (capture 
et d’IA) 
- 
il permet d’estimer les points de référence pour la gestion basée sur des 
points d’équilibre à long terme comme le MSY (Maximum Sustainable Yield) et 
EMSY (le niveau d’exploitation correspondant). 
Les modèles globaux sont généralement critiqués du fait de leur simplicité et des 
résultats absurdes auxquels leur utilisation a parfois conduit (faute d’une expertise 
appropriée), les modèles structurés en âge leur étant généralement préférés (Hilborn 
et Walters, 1992). Cependant, des études comparatives entre les deux types 
d’approches ont permis de réhabiliter les modèles globaux en montrant leur intérêt et 
leur cohérence en terme d’évaluation de stock par rapport aux approches 
analytiques (Ludwig et Walters, 1985). De plus, ces modèles sont toujours utilisés, 
notamment au sein de la majorité des commissions thonières, du fait des difficultés 
inhérentes à l’âgeage et à la collecte des données dans de nombreuses pêcheries 
tropicales (Hilborn et Walters, 1992). 
La productivité biologique apparaît ainsi comme dépendante de l'abondance de la 
population. On parle à ce sujet de phénomènes densité-dépendants ou de 
mécanismes de régulation densité dépendants. Les modèles globaux peuvent ainsi 
                                                          
2 Ce processus de fonctionnement est assuré par les mécanismes de régulation densité-dépendant. Une fois la capacité 
biotique atteinte, l’abondance du stock ne reste évidemment pas parfaitement constante. Dans la réalité, en plus du fait que les 
paramètres estimés par le modèle global ne sont que des valeurs moyennes (des espérances au sens mathématique du 
terme), se rajoutent donc des fluctuations irrégulières, liées au climat, à l’environnement biotique ou abiotique. On peut ainsi 
imaginer qu’un stock est en permanence en évolution, à la poursuite d’un équilibre qu’il n’atteint jamais (Gascuel, 2008). 


60 
 
être considérés comme des modèles de compétition intra-spécifique, basés sur 
l'existence de mécanismes de régulation densité dépendants (Gascuel 2008).  
o Formulationgénérale du modèle 
Les modèles de production de biomasse s’expriment en spécifiant une relation 
directe entre la production biologique d’un stock (

biotique et m un paramètre de forme dans le modèle de Pella et Tomlinson.  
o Modèle à l’équilibre 
L’idée de base d’un modèle global est qu’une ressource renouvelable exploitée voit 
son rendement à l’équilibre varier en fonction de son exploitation. La capture totale 
augmente progressivement avec l’augmentation de l’effort de pêche, passe par un 
maximum, puis décline rapidement avant de tendre vers zéro quand le stock est 
surexploité. Au maximum de cette courbe de production à l’équilibre se trouve le 
point de MSY et l'effort EMSY correspondant. Ce dernier est exprimé ici en 
multiplicateur d’effort (mEMSY) par rapport à la moyenne des trois dernières années. 
En pratique, la série historique des données disponibles doit couvrir la période 
d’expansion de la pêcherie, afin d'observer la réponse du stock à l’accroissement de 
l’effort de pêche. Notons que le modèle raisonne sur un seul stock, en supposant 
l'existence de mécanismes de régulation qui permettent le maintien du stock à 
l’équilibre. 


61 
 
 
Figure 19: Evolution de la PUE à l’équilibre (a) et de la captures à l’équilibre (b) en fonction 
de l’effort de pêche, pour différentes valeurs du paramètre de forme (m) du modèle 
généralisé de Pella et Tomlinson (in Gascuel 2008) 
 
o Modèle dynamique 
Le modèle dynamique est une variante du modèle global et utilise son 
équation discrétisée, sur un pas de temps annuel (équation). Ceci suppose que 
l’abondance d’une année donnée dépend de l’abondance de l’année précédente.  
 
  1) = )  ) 1 −)
  −) 
 
Notons que le modèle dynamique reste basé sur l’hypothèse fonctionnelle 
d’équilibre dans la mesure où la fonction de production incorporée dans son équation 
de base est la même que précédemment, ainsi que les formules des points de 
référence qui en découlent (MSY, mfMSY ….). Cette hypothèse suppose l'existence 
de mécanismes de régulation interne au stock et implique qu'un effort de pêche 
constant conduirait à des valeurs de biomasse et de production correspondant aux 
valeurs d'équilibre. En revanche, les abondances observées chaque année dans le 
modèle dynamique ne sont plus supposées être à l’équilibre et le modèle est donc 
moins contraignant en terme d'hypothèse. Mais ceci s'accompagne d'une plus 
grande flexibilité du modèle qui a pour contre partie un sur-paramétrage, 4 
paramètres contre 2 à l’équilibre ; la capturabilité (q) et la biomasse à l’état initial(B0) 
en plus de r et K. Ce qui peut poser problème dans l’ajustement du modèle si les 
données ne sont pas assez informatives. Cette nouvelle difficulté peut être au moins 


62 
 
partiellement résolue en recourant à un cadre d'ajustement Bayesien, dans lequel 
des connaissances et des contraintes sont introduites sous forme de priors.  
 
1.4.4. Ajustement du modèle global dans un cadre Bayesien 
 
Le cadre Bayesien offre un cadre flexible de modélisation et permet de 
quantifier proporement les incertitudes autour des estimations des paramètres et 
dans les diagnostics. L’utilisation du modèle dynamique dans un cadre bayesien 
permet la prise en compte des connaissances expertes dans la construction à priori 
des paramétres du modèle.  Le cadre statistique Bayesien est entièrement basé sur 
la manipulation de distributions de probabilité. Il est en ce sens mathématiquement 
rigoureux, et adapté pour la prise en compte des incertitudes et des risques dans 
l’évaluation et la gestion des ressources halieutiques (Punt et Hilborn 1997 ; 
McAllister et Kirkwood 1998 ; Harwood et Stockes 2003, in Ono et al. 2012).  
En notant θ, le vecteur des inconnues du modèle, la distribution a posteriori de θ, 
notée p(θ|Data), s’obtient en combinant la distribution a priori p(θ) et la distribution 
conditionnelle des données p(Data|θ) (= vraisemblance) par la formule de Bayes : 
 
|é ) =
é |). )
" é |) . )# 
L’analyse Bayesienne permet ainsi de passer du prior au posterior grâce à une mise 
à jour apportée par les données via le modèle.

Figure 20: Schéma précisant le principe de l’approche bayesienne (in Ono, 2007) 
 
L’inférence Bayesienne doit être comprise comme étant dépendante de la 
distribution a priori qui a  été choisie. Si on part avec un “mauvais” a priori, on obtient 
de mauvais résultats. Par contre, parmi les avantages de l’inférence Bayesienne ce 
qu’elle tient automatiquement compte de l’incertitude due au nombre d’exemples 
dans les intervalles de confiance qu’elle nous donne quant aux prédictions faites. 
Deux types d'incertitude, l'erreur de processus et l'erreur d'observation, 
peuvent expliquer les écarts entre valeurs observées et valeurs prédites. De 
nombreux auteurs montrent que dans le cas de modèles dynamiques, la prise en 
compte simultanée des deux sources d’incertitude dans le cadre théorique des 
modèles stochastiques à états latents (state-space models ou SSM) permet en 
moyenne d’obtenir les estimations les moins biaisées (Punt 2003 ; Meyer et Millar 
1999 ; Millar et Meyer 2000).  
 
1.4.5. Vers une approche écosystèmiques 
 
Les approches conventionnelles en gestion des pêches se focalisent essentiellement 
sur les espèces d’intérêt commercial, en considérant chaque stock de manière 
indépendante. Leur objectif général est de concilier l’exploitation halieutique et la 
conservation 
des 
ressources,alors 
qu’il 
s’agit 
d’une 
question 
à 
l’échelle 
écosystèmique par le biais des perturbations engendrées. La pêche a un impact 
direct sur les populations ciblées, particulièrement les espèces à durée de vie longue 
et faible taux de reproduction (p. ex. Jennings et al., 1998). Elle a également des 
effets indirects pouvant être très importants sur la structure des communautés, les 
interactions trophiques, la faune benthique et l’habitat (Jennings et Kaiser, 1998 ; 
Hall, 1999 ; Gislason, 2003). A titre d’illustration, la surexploitation d’espèces de 
poissons consommatrices d’oursins herbivores dans les écosystèmes coralliens de 
Jamaïque a conduit à une explosion de l’abondance des oursins, et a eu pour 
conséquence une diminution drastique des macroalgues (Hughes, 1994). Les effets 
indirects de la pêche peuvent ainsi avoir des impacts plus importants sur la structure 
et la dynamique des écosystèmes marins que la suppression des poissons eux-
mêmes (Botsford et al., 1997). 


64 
 
La prise en compte d’une dimension écosystémique en aménagement des pêcheries 
a deux objectifs principaux : (i) améliorer la compréhension des dynamiques des 
populations et des écosystèmes en ayant une vision globale des différents facteurs 
impliqués (ii) mieux quantifier les impacts de la pêche qui ne se limitent pas aux 
espèces exploitées mais affectent la structure et le fonctionnement des écosystèmes 
(Chassot 2005). 
1.4.6. Vulnérabilités des espèces ou des écosystèmes 
La vulnérabilité des espèces à la pêche dépend de leurs caractéristiques vitales ainsi 
que de l’extension de leur aire géographique. Au plan des caractéristiques 
biologiques, les espèces à taux de fécondité et de croissance élevés, qui sont des 
formes de petite taille et de faible longévité, sont moins vulnérables à l’exploitation 
que les formes de grande taille et de forte longévité, qui ont une faible fécondité et 
une faible croissance. Par ailleurs, des cas patents de changements de structures 
par l’érosion ou l’altération du réseau trophique sous l’effet de la pêche ont été 
démontrés (Pauly et al., 1998). 
• Approche comparative et modélisation écosystémique :  
La fonction de réponse des différents stocks, à la pression de pêche croissante 
intervenue en Mauritanie, sera comparée en recourant à différents outils d'analyse : 
tests statistiques des relations entre indicateurs de l'état des stocks et paramètres 
démographiques ou niveau trophique, analyse des relations entre fonction de 
réponse et écologie ou habitat des espèces, positionnement des différents stocks sur 
des graphiques de synthèse pression de pêche/abondance relative, etc. 
 
1.4.7. ‘Ecopath with Ecosim’ et EcoTroph 
 
• Principe et intérêt du modèle Ecopath 
Le modèle Ecopath est utilisé comme un outil efficace pour étudier les 
caractéristiques des écosystèmes. Il permet de représenter simplement la complexité 
d'un écosystème, et d’apporter une synthése cohérentes des connaissances. 
Cette approche utilise les principes d'équilibre de masse pour estimer les flux 
(Christensen et Pauly, 1992). Le modèle comporte les différents groupes fonctionnels 


65 
 
constituant l’écosystème et chaque groupe est représenté par une équation 
d'équilibre de masse et nécessite six paramètres d'entrée : biomasse (B), production 
(P), consommation (Q), efficacité écotrophique (EE; la fraction de la production qui 
est soit utilisée dans le réseau trophique, soit exportée), la composition du régime 
alimentaire et les captures commerciales de chaque groupe. Les équations linéaires 
sont résolues par algèbre matricielle pour produire des estimations des flux qui 
équilibrent les entrées et les sorties ; tout paramètre manquant fait l'objet d'une 
estimation (EE est estimée si tous les paramètres ont été entrés).  
• Construction d’un modèle Ecopath Mauritanie 
En interaction avec le travail d’un post-doctorant, l'impact de la pêche à l’échelle de 
l’écosystème Mauritanien a pu être analysé à travers un modèle Ecopath construit en 
étroite collaboration avec le doctorant. Le principe du modèle Ecopath et les étapes 
de construction du modèle Ecopath et Ecosim Mauritanie sont abordés dans le 
quatrième chapitre. 
• Spectres trophiques et modèle EcoTroph 
La construction des spectres trophiques de biomasse et de capture est basée sur la 
connaissance du niveau trophique moyen de chaque espèce et de la variabilité de ce 
niveau trophique au sein de chaque stock. Ces données peuvent être estimées en 
sortie d'un modèle écosystémique de type Ecopath. Les spectres ainsi obtenus 
constituent un outil extrêmement synthétique de représentation de l'état de 
l'écosystème et  de son exploitation. Ils permettent notamment d'évaluer la pression 
de pêche exercée et son impact sur la biomasse de l'écosystème, à chaque niveau 
trophique. Ces spectres sont également la variable d'entrée du modèle EcoTroph qui 
permet de simuler des changements de diagramme d'exploitation et d'en analyser les 
effets à l'échelle de l'écosystème. Ce modèle vise en outre à fournir un diagnostic et 
des indicateurs de gestion, non plus stock par stock, mais pour l'ensemble des 
compartiments de l'écosystème.  
Dans le cadre de cette thèse, on cherchera ainsi à établir un diagnostic à l'échelle de 
la ZEE de Mauritanie et analyser l'impact de la réserve du Banc d'Arguin. 
Pour estimer l’impact de la pression de pêche sur la communauté de poissons 
démersaux, trois aspects seront abordés. En premier lieux, les possibles 


66 
 
changements de la structure trophique sont considérés en comparant la dynamique 
du spectre trophique de la biomasse et des captures à différentes périodes. En 
second lieu, les tendances des niveaux trophiques moyens des captures sont 
comparées. En fin, une simulation par EcoTroph permettra d’analyser plusieurs 
scénarios de changement du système et les mesures de gestion adéquates.

CHAPITRE 2 : DES MODELES D’EVALUATION DES 
STOCKS POUR QUANTIFIER L’IMPACT DE LA PECHE 
SUR LE STOCK DU MEROU BLANC (EPINEPHELUS 
AENEUS) EN MAURITANIE 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


75 
 
Résumé du chapitre 
Ce chapitre présente une approche multi méthode d’évaluation des stocks dans un 
contexte dit « data poor », qui caractérise les ressources et les pêcheries 
mauritaniennes, au moins en termes de qualité des données disponibles. Un des 
principaux intérêts de cette approche est de montrer comment l’analyse de diverses 
sources de données et l’utilisation de plusieurs méthodes mises en œuvre de 
manière indépendante permettent de renforcer le diagnostic d’évaluation de stock, 
même dans un contexte de faible qualité des données. L’approche mise en œuvre 
permet de valoriser l’ensemble des données disponibles notamment les données de 
campagnes scientifiques. L’une des contributions significatives de cette étude réside 
également dans l’utilisation de modèles de production de biomasse intégrant une 
non stationnarité de la productivité en fonction des variations inter-annuelles de 
l’indice d’upwelling. 
Dans le cas du mérou blanc de Mauritanie (Epinephelus aeneus), la mise en œuvre 
de cette approche permet de montrer qu’avec deux modèles d’évaluation 
conceptuellement différents (l’un analytique structuré en âges et l’autre global) et en 
s’appuyant sur des données indépendantes (structures démographiques dans un 
cas, série historiques de captures et d’abondances dans l’autre) on aboutit à des 
diagnostics proches l’un de l’autre et globalement très cohérents. Cette convergence 
des diagnostics conforte évidement la fiabilité qu’on peut accorder à chacun d’eux. 
Elle permet ici de conclure que le stock de mérou blanc de Mauritanie est surexploité 
de manière très significative. Les diagnostics confirment l’excédent d’effort de pêche 
sur cette espèce avec des chiffres allant de 30% à 50% par rapport à l’effort actuel.  
Cette fourchette concorde avec celle estimée pour cette espèce au Sénégal, où les 
excédents estimés par Laurans (2005) varient entre 35 et 55%. L’abondance de 
cette espèce est en déclin sur toute la période avec un léger redressement depuis 
2006. La biomasse moyenne estimée au milieu des années quatre vingt est quatre 
fois supérieure à celle estimée pour les années 2008-2009. En outre, les poissons 
les plus exploités sont ceux d’âges compris entre 4 et 10 ans, avec une mortalité 
maximale d’environ 0.8 an-1 chez les poissons de 8 ans. Pour une espèce qui vit 
plus de 15 ans, la pêche s’exerce à l’état actuel sur des poissons de relativement 
petites tailles.  


76 
 
L’analyse globale des différents diagnostics obtenus, ainsi qu’une démarche experte 
basée sur l’expérience concrète de l’évaluation, conduisent en outre à choisir une 
méthode de référence, qui sera utilisée dans le chapitre III. C’est ici le modèle 
dynamique de production de biomasse, incluant un effet de l’environnement, qui 
semble produire les résultats les plus fiables, résultats qui sont en quelque sorte 
confortés par les autres modèles. Plus généralement, la présente étude montre que 
le croisement des résultats issus de différentes méthodes aboutit à une évaluation 
suffisamment robuste pour qu’elle puisse être utilisée pour la définition de mesures 
concrètes d’aménagement des pêches.   
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


African Journal of Marine Science 2013, 35(2): 253–267

AFRICAN JOURNAL OF
MARINE SCIENCE
ISSN 1814-232X   EISSN 1814-2338
http://dx.doi.org/10.2989/1814232X.2013.798244
African Journal of Marine Science is co-published by NISC (Pty) Ltd and Taylor & Francis
West Africa is characterised by rapidly developing fisheries. 
Most of the fishery resources in this region, particularly 
coastal demersal fish species, were not intensively exploited 
until the 1960s (FAO 1986, Garibaldi and Grainger 2005, 
Gascuel et al. 2007a). However, fishing effort has increased 
rapidly in the past few decades (Chavance 2005), leading to 
a large decrease in biomass and to signs of severe overex-
ploitation (Christensen et al. 2005, Gascuel et al. 2005). 
Species exhibiting large sizes, slow growth rates and late 
sexual maturity, which render them more vulnerable to 
fishing mortality (Gislason and Rice 1998), appear to be 
the most affected by fisheries. In particular, Gascuel et al. 
(2007b) showed that the abundance of demersal resources 
in the northern part of West Africa has decreased fourfold 
over the last 20–30 years.
One of the most affected species is the white grouper 
Epinephelus aeneus — locally referred to as ‘thiof’ — 
which is a species of high commercial value throughout 
West Africa. In Senegal, this species sustains intensive 
exploitation mainly due to the artisanal fishery. Previous 
stock assessments revealed that the Senegal stock might 
be in a state of high overexploitation associated with an 
excess fishing effort of 45% compared to the effort at 
maximum sustainable yield (EMSY), and that biomass was 
reduced to 15% of virgin levels (Laurans et al. 2003, Sidibé 
2003, Gascuel et al. 2005). Moreover, a large decrease in 
numbers of mature individuals has been observed, along 
with a reduction of the population’s geographic distribution 
(Balguerias et al. 2000 in Sidibé 2010). According to the 
International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN), this 
species faces a high risk of extinction if the same pressures 
are applied in the future (Sidibé 2010). 
In Mauritania (Figure 1a), only partial analyses have been 
conducted on E. aeneus. Several studies have shown a large 
reduction in the abundance of this species (Erzini et al. 2005, 
Failler et al. 2006, Gascuel et al. 2007b) but without any stock 
assessment based on a population dynamics model, leading 
to an empirical understanding of the stock status. Thus, it 
is necessary to develop a quantitative assessment to guide 
fisheries management. More broadly, a lack of reliable indica-
tors of the condition of resources is another fairly common 
characteristic of West African fisheries. This situation is 
firstly linked to the weak structure of the research system 
in the region and, consequently, the absence of a stable 
fishery monitoring system over the long-term. Available data, 
either from statistics based on captures and fishery efforts 
or from scientific sampling, are often incomplete, have been 
established for short time-series, or were collected according 
to heterogeneous protocols (Chavance et al. 2005, Gascuel 
et al. 2007b, Labrosse et al. 2010). 
In addition to the issue of data quality, catches are 
highly multispecific and exploitation methods are highly 
Assessing stocks in data-poor African fisheries: a case study on the white 
grouper Epinephelus aeneus of Mauritania
B Meissa1,2, D Gascuel2* and E Rivot2
1 Institut Mauritanien de Recherches Océanographiques et des Pêches (IMROP), BP 22, Nouadhibou, Mauritanie
2 Université Européenne de Bretagne, Agrocampus Ouest, UMR985 ESE Ecologie et santé des écosystèmes, 65 route de 
Saint Brieuc, CS 84215, 35 042 Rennes Cedex, France
* Corresponding author, e-mail: didier.gascuel@agrocampus-ouest.fr
The lack of reliable stock assessment for numerous exploited stocks in West Africa often results from poor-quality 
data, high multi-specificity of captures, and the heterogeneity of exploitation methods. However, many signs of 
overexploitation exist, particularly for demersal resources, highlighting the urgent need for a more quantitative 
and comprehensive evaluation of these resources. This study aims to show how, in such a context of poor-quality 
data and high uncertainty, a multi-method approach for stock assessment can generate a consistent diagnosis 
of the condition of a resource. As a case study, several methods were combined to assess the stock status 
of the white grouper Epinephelus aeneus, a flagship species in West Africa that is exploited by industrial and 
small-scale fisheries in Mauritania. These were estimation of abundance indices using delta generalised linear 
models; a biomass production model using a pseudo-equilibrium method and including an environmental effect 
of upwelling intensity; a dynamic biomass production model fitted in a Bayesian framework also including 
an environmental effect; and an age-structured model based on a modified pseudo-cohort analysis. Sensitivity 
analyses were performed for most of these assessment methods. Results show that the white grouper stock is 
highly overexploited due to an excess in the fishing effort estimated at between 30% and 50%, depending on the 
model used to estimate the effort at maximum sustainable yield.
Keywords: age-structured model, Bayesian approaches, diagnosis, production model, stock assessment
Introduction
Downloaded by [197.231.27.88] at 02:53 14 August 2013 


Meissa, Gascuel and Rivot
254
heterogeneous. West African fisheries, particularly those 
off Mauritania, are characterised by the existence of two 
very different types of fisheries: industrial fisheries, which 
generally use trawls and involve foreign funds, and artisanal 
small-scale fisheries, which deploy a large number of 
dugout canoes with highly diverse types of fishing gear.
In this paper, we show that these difficulties can be 
at least partially overcome by combining different stock 
assessment methods, to take advantage of all available 
data and provide an evaluation of stock status that can 
be useful to define management measures. Epinephelus 
aeneus in Mauritania is used as a case study to illustrate 
the multi-method stock assessment approach. Several 
approaches are implemented: (1) assessment of standard-
ised abundance indices using delta generalised linear model 
(delta-GLM) methods (Stefansson 1996, Le Pape et al. 
2003, Laurans 2005); (2) a biomass production model fitted 
using the pseudo-equilibrium method (Pella and Tomlinson 
1969), and including non-stationarity of parameters with 
regards to environmental effects (Fréon 1991, Thiaw et al. 
2009); (3) a dynamic biomass production model fitted in 
a Bayesian framework (Punt and Hilborn 1997, Ono et al. 
2012) and also including environmental effects; and (4) an 
age-structured method based on a modified pseudo-cohort 
analysis (Laurec and Santarelli-Chaurand 1986, Chassot et 
al. 2008a) and on a model of biomass and yield-per-recruit 
(Beverton and Holt 1957).
Material and methods
Data
The Mauritanian stock of E. aeneus is considered in the 
present study as a single stock covering the whole Mauritanian 
coast. In fact, adults migrate from the Cap Blanc on the 
northern border of Mauritania to the cap Roxo in south 
Senegal (Champagnat and Domain 1978). Thus, E. 
aeneus from Mauritania and Senegal are sometimes 
assumed to belong to the same stock (Laurans et al. 
2003). Nevertheless, mainly due to practical reasons (i.e. 
the difficulty of merging data from these two countries), 
all attempts to assess the stock status have been, until 
recently, conducted on a national basis. Assessing E. 
aeneus in Mauritania as a single stock is a pragmatic 
approach aimed at providing knowledge complementary 
to that currently available in Senegal (Laurans et al. 2003, 
Gascuel et al. 2005, Thiao et al. 2012).
Two series of annual abundance indices were esti-
mated using linear models, one from the catch per unit 
effort (CPUE) of a small-scale fishery and the other from 
scientific surveys. Catch and effort statistics of the small-
scale fishery were estimated by the Mauritanian Institute of 
Research (IMROP), based on observer data covering the 
landing sites along the Mauritanian coast since 1997. The 
sampling scheme is stratified by month, gear and fishing 
area (the sampling protocol is detailed in Failler et al. 
2006 and Labrosse et al. 2010). The CPUE values were 
calculated per month and per fishing area, dividing handline 
catches of E. aeneus (which account for more than 80% 
of the small-scale catch of the species) by the observed 
fishing effort, expressed as the number of fishing trips in a 
given fishing area. Scientific surveys take into account all 
of the demersal surveys undertaken along the Mauritanian 
continental shelf between 1982 and 2009, totalling 63 
surveys and 5 282 trawls. Each observation corresponds 
to one trawl with an average horizontal opening of 17 m 
dragged at a trawl speed of 3.2 knots, for a standard 
Figure 1: (a) the Mauritanian study area; (b) trends in the catch of Epinephelus aeneus for the industrial and small-scale fisheries, and

duration of 30 min. The fishing gear was modified in 1989 
and hence the correction coefficients estimated by Gascuel 
et al. (2007b), using data from inter-calibration surveys 
reported by Girardin et al. (1990), were applied to data 
gathered prior to that date.
Total catches used in biomass production models 
were derived from the small-scale and industrial fisheries 
(Figure 1b). For the small-scale fishery, the IMROP 
database covers the period 1997–2010; hence, data for 
the years 1982–1996 were reconstructed based on the 
number of dugout canoes per year (Gascuel et al. 2007a) 
and the average catch per canoe. Catches from the 
industrial fishery were estimated from IMROP fishery data 
aggregated by commercial categories, where E. aeneus 
was included in the ‘various demersal finfish’ category. 
These data are available for the period 1991–2010 and 
have been disaggregated by species using specific 
composition profiles obtained through the at-sea observa-
tion programme. Epinephelus aeneus catches were extrap-
olated for the period 1982–1990 in proportion to the number 
of industrial vessels. 
The catch-at-age used for virtual population analysis 
(VPA) was estimated for an average year representative of 
the recent period from total catches per size class, which are 
available for the period 2007–2009 (IMROP database). The 
finite mixture distribution model (Mixdist) of the R package 
(Du 2002, Chassot et al. 2008b) was used to convert 
catch-at-length into catch-at-age, under the assumption of 
a known growth curve according to the method proposed 
by Gascuel (1994). For each age class a, a sum of normal 
distributions with fixed mean size at age La and standard 
deviation Va was fitted to catch-at-length data. Mean length-
at-age was derived from a previously established growth 
model (Santamaría at al. 2010) and Va was derived from the 
empirical model; Va   D  EÂ'La, where 'La represents the 
size increment at age a. 
Finally, a time-series of the annual upwelling index 
(denoted by w below) was used to explore the role of 
environmental fluctuations on the population dynamics of 
E. aeneus. The role of upwelling in the bottom-up control 
of marine ecosystems is well established: intensification of 
the trade wind generates upwelling of deep, nutrient-rich 
waters to the surface and results in optimal environmental 
conditions for plankton (Cury and Roy 1989, Bakun 1990, 
Cushing 1990). The upwelling index was calculated from the 
Ekman transport equation in the wind database (IMROP) 
covering the period 1980–2009. 
Estimation of abundance indices using generalised 
linear models 
To estimate a series of abundance indices from the small-
scale data, a linear model was fitted to the log-transformed 
CPUE. This log transformation accounts for the multiplica-
tive nature of the effects being studied and homogenises 
the variance. The following factors were considered in the 
analysis (Eqn 1): year, season (cold: January–May; hot: 
August–October; and the two intermediate seasons) and 
region (north: Banc d’Arguin; central: Nouakchott; and 
south):

Fitting the model under the pseudo-equilibrium hypothesis
The generalised biomass production model and the Fox model 
can be fitted to abundance and fishing effort data using the 
pseudo-equilibrium method proposed by Fox (1970). This 
method, though sometimes criticised (Hilborn and Walters 
1992), is still used in stock assessment groups because of its 
robustness (Fonteneau et al. 1998, Gascuel 2008, Chassot 
et al. 2010). It assumes that the stock abundance observed 
during year t (IAt
combined) is in equilibrium with a linear combina-
tion of fishing effort values from k previous years. This 
combination, noted as mf here, is referred to as the ‘Fox effort’. 
The hypothesis of pseudo-equilibrium is then expressed as:

Parameters (a,b) and (aƍ,bƍ) were estimated by fitting

identically log-normally distributed.
All other parameters were considered known, and 
sensitivity analyses were conducted to assess how results 
are sensitive to the values of these parameters. Three 
different values of the shape parameter m were tested: 
m   0.3, 0.5 and 0.9. Analyses were also performed using 
different values of the number of age classes considered 
in the Fox effort (k), ranging between 3 and 5. Different 
hypotheses for the trends in fishing power were tested (D   
0% optimistic, 5%, or 10% pessimistic). Three different 
periods for the time-series were considered: 1982–2009, 
1985–2009 and 1991–2009.
Fitting the dynamic production model within the Bayesian 
framework
The dynamic biomass production model was fitted to the 
same abundance index data (IAt
combined) and catches in the 
Bayesian framework. Only the Fox model was considered 
in this analysis. The dynamic model relaxes the hypothesis 
of pseudo-equilibrium, but more parameters are estimated, 
which could lead to identifiability issues (Gascuel 2008). 
The model was written in its discrete approximation on 
an annual time step, and implemented within a stochastic 
state–space framework (McAllister and Kirkwood 1998, 
Meyer and Millar 1999, Ono et al. 2012). The stochastic 
process deduces the biomass at time t+1 from the biomass 
at time t, taking into account a process error around the 
dynamic equation of the model:

An informative prior distribution was considered for the

butions. Also, the initial biomass B0 (for the first year of the 
series, 1985) was set at half the carrying capacity (K; see 
Table 1). 
A sensitivity analysis to the prior used for r was 
conducted by considering five different prior distributions 
selected within a reasonable range (Table 1). The distri-
bution centred on r   1 was taken as a reference. For E. 
aeneus, this distribution corresponds to the value calculated 
in FishBase (http://www.fishbase.org) using an empirical 
equation from Ricker (1975), expressing r as a function of 
Fmax, where Fmax is derived from a yield-per-recruit model 
(Beverton and Holt 1957). The same value (r   1) was also 
estimated based on a matrix model applied to the species in 
Tunisia (Miled et al. 2010).
All computations were performed within the R platform 
(www.Rproject.org). Bayesian posterior distributions of 
all model parameters and unknown variables (e.g. the 
time-series of biomass) were approximated via Monte Carlo

Markov Chain (MCMC) methods through the open-source 
OpenBUGS® software (Thomas et al. 2006, Lunn et al. 
2009). Three MCMC-independent chains with dispersed 
initialisation points were used. For each chain, the first 
100 000 iterations were discarded. After this ‘burn-in’ period, 
inferences were derived from a sample of 3 u 400 000 
iterations. One out of 10 iterations was kept to reduce the 
MCMC sampling autocorrelation. All the modelling results 
have undergone the Gelman-Rubin test (Brooks and Gelman 
1998) as implemented in the Coda package of R to assess 
convergence of MCMC chains.
The quality of fit was assessed using the deviance 
information criterion (DIC) (Spiegelhalter et al. 2002), which 
can be interpreted as a Bayesian analogue of the Akaike 
information criterion (AIC).
Including an upwelling effect in biomass production models
The effect of environment on the biomass dynamic was 
tested in both the dynamic and the pseudo-equilibrium 
approach.

Corrected analysis of a pseudo-cohort: a model of the 
biomass and yield-per-recruit
Age-structured models are usually used for fisheries where 
catches-at-length or catches-at-age are estimated on a 
regular basis (throughout the years), which is not often the 
case in West Africa. The analysis of a pseudo-cohort can be 
achieved from data observed during a single year, or from a 
small number of years that can be considered representative 
(an average year). Nevertheless, this analysis assumes that 
the recruitment and fishing effort are constant throughout 
the previous years, leading to biased results when these 
assumptions are not met. In particular, when the fishing 
effort increases, fishing mortality is underestimated. This can 
lead to an exaggeratedly optimistic assessment, possibly 
resulting in severe consequences for fisheries management 
(Chassot et al. 2008a). 
To relax this assumption, a corrected pseudo-cohort 
analysis was implemented, as proposed by Laurec and 
Santarelli-Chaurand (1986), using a program developed in 
R by Chassot et al (2008a). This corrected analysis takes 
into account the past changes occurring in the fishing effort. 
It was initiated here based on the following parameters:
• The vector [Ct,Y] for captures at age t for the average of 
2007–2009, which was considered to be the last year of 
the analysis and was indexed as Y 
• The natural mortality M is assumed to be constant across 
years and ages. This mortality was estimated to be 
between 0.2 and 0.3 in Senegal (Cury and Worms 1982). 
A sensitivity analysis was conducted for values of M  
0.20, 0.25 and 0.30, with M   0.25 as a reference
• The fishing efforts (Ey), from previous years. The series 
used here was obtained from handline activities from 
the small-scale fisheries for the period 1997–2009. This 
series was assumed to be representative of the total 
fishing effort of the fishery.
The corrected pseudo-cohort analysis relies on three 
basic equations used to estimate the size and the fishing 
mortality of the cohort for each age, based on an ascending 
iterative process. The catch equation is given as follows:

The equation for separability is based on a hypothesis 
of constant catchability over the years and is expressed as 
follows:
 
 
 
Ft,y   qtEy 
 
(25)
where Ct,y, Nt,y and Ft,y represent the catch, number of fish 
and fishing mortality respectively, at age t and for year y, 
qt is the catchability at age t, and Ey is the fishing effort for 
year y. 
The calculations are initialised from an average value of 
recruitment (R) used for each of the years Y–T through Y. 
Thus, for the final year (Y) the recruitment is known (N1,Y  
R), and therefore the fishing mortality F1,Y is derived from 
the catch equation (Eqn 23). Then, based on the equation 
for separability (Eqn 25), we can infer q1, the catchability at 
age 1. That same equation is used to calculate the fishing 
mortality F1,Y–1 for year Y–1, from which we infer the number 
of fish at age 2 for the final year N2,Y, based on the equation 
for survival (Eqn 24). The same process is repeated, 
successively estimating the mortality F2,Y (Eqn 23), the 
K
Downloaded by [197.231.27.88] at 02:53 14 August 2013 


African Journal of Marine Science 2013, 35(2): 253–267
259
catchability q2 (Eqn 25), the mortality from the previous year 
F2,Y–1 (Eqn 25), and the number of fish at age 3 (N3,Y). The 
process is then repeated for each age group, thus giving 
number of fish for each age in each cohort for the years Y–T 
through Y, assuming that all groups had the same recruit-
ment (R) but experienced different yearly fishing effort (Ey).
Presented in this way, the corrected pseudo-cohorts 
analysis implies that the calculations begin with a value of 
recruitment and lead to a mortality estimate for the terminal 
age, FT,Y. In practice, the program developed by Chassot et 
al. (2008a) requires the user to introduce a terminal value of 
FT,Y and then search for the corresponding recruitment via 
iteration for the whole analysis. 
The obtained fishing mortalities were compared to those 
of a simple pseudo-cohort analysis (i.e. not corrected by 
past changes in the fishing effort) and used to calculate a 
model of biomass and yield-per-recruit following Beverton 
and Holt (1957). The values of Y/R and B/R are therefore 
calculated for fishing mortality multipliers ranging from 0 to 
2. These models are commonly used in fisheries science 
(Sparre and Venema 1992), so they are not detailed here. 
Results
Time-series of abundance indices 
Both submodels, the presence/absence (IA0/1) and non-null 
observation submodels (IA+), fitted to scientific survey data, 
indicate the existence of significant interannual change 
(Table 2). The residuals from the submodel for positive 
CPUE do not depart from the assumptions of normality 
and homogeneity of variance. The model for presence/
absence shows that E. aeneus is encountered increas-
ingly less frequently during scientific surveys (Figure 2). Its 
probability of occurrence in the trawls in the early 1980s 
was three times higher than during the period 2007–2009. 
The estimated average of positive densities also exhibits 
a clear decreasing trend. Combining the two components 
results in a drastic decline of E. aeneus abundance over 
the entire investigated period. There was an increased 
decline starting in 1995, then stabilisation at fairly low levels 
beginning in 1997, with a slight improvement since 2006. 
The same accelerated drop from 1995 to 1997 is also found 
for the abundance index of the artisanal fishery. Similar to 
the scientific surveys, the evolution of the abundance index 
for the artisanal fishery during the past decade shows a 
stabilisation at fairly low yield levels. 
Biomass production model fitted under the pseudo-
equilibrium hypothesis
When the Fox production model is analysed under the 
pseudo-equilibrium hypothesis, the correlations (r2) between 
the observed abundance indices (IAt
combined) and those 
predicted by the models (Îe, at a stable state) are highly 
significant (r2   0.87; Figure 3a and Table 3). The indicators 
of stock status show a strong reduction in biomass as well 
as overexploitation of the stock since 2001. The maximum 
sustainable yield (MSY) is estimated to be 922 t for this 
reference model.
The overall index of overexploitation is robust to change 
in the values of fixed parameters, leading to an estimated 
excess in the fishing effort of approximately 40% (the 
effort multiplier at MSY is mEMSY   0.58). The various fitted

African Journal of Marine Science 2013, 35(2): 253–267
261
models are not very sensitive to the choice of the period 
(1982–2009, 1985–2009 or 1991–2009). Nevertheless, the 
diagnosis is less severe when the analysis is performed 
solely for the recent period, excluding the period of high 
depletion of the stock (Figure 3b). The estimates of MSY 
vary by 10% depending on the hypothesis of the trend 
in the fishing power (Figure 3c). This hypothesis also 
influences the diagnosis, with the excess in the fishing effort 
being estimated to be 25% when no drift in fishing power is 
assumed for the small-scale fishery. The results are also 
particularly sensitive to the shape parameter (m) of the 
Pella and Tomlinson model (Figure 3d); values of m smaller 
than 1 lead to better correlation compared to the Fox model 
and to less pessimistic or even optimistic conclusions. 
Integration of the environmental upwelling effect (Fréon 
model) significantly improves the quality of fit (Table 3, Figure 
3e). The analysis with several time-lag scenarios shows 
that the relationship between the residuals and the environ-
mental index is only significant for time lags ranging from 0 
to 2 years, the best fit being obtained with a time lag of 2 
years (wn–2). This suggests that upwelling, in addition to its 
direct influence upon the abundance of this species through 
affecting its seasonal migrations in Mauritanian–Senegalese 
waters, is also expected to have an impact on recruitment, 
which is experienced with a time lag of 2 years. This model 
indicates an excess fishing effort of 40% and estimates 
a MSY equal to 924 t under average upwelling conditions, 
varying from 1 129 to 714 t respectively, for minimal and 
maximal upwelling values within the observed series. 
Dynamic biomass production model
The assimilation of capture and abundance index data in the 
dynamic model enables us to update the priors (Figure 4). 
The intrinsic rate of increase (r) is estimated to be 0.98 
(post-median), and the carrying capacity (K) is estimated to 
be 23 290 t (Table 3). The sensitivity analysis shows that 
these estimations of the model’s parameters r and K are not 
very sensitive to the prior used for r or to the initial depletion 
rate of the biomass (Figure 5). 
The estimated abundance index (IAt
combined) fit well to the 
observed indices (Figure 6). The estimated effort multiplier 
at MSY (mEMSY) represents 0.61 of the actual effort (39% 
excess fishing effort). Based on a 75% Bayesian credible 
interval, the mEMSY ranges between 0.5 and 0.7 (Table 3). 
The dynamic model confirms that the stock is overex-
ploited, and the diagnosis is very close to that obtained 
with the pseudo-equilibrium model. The MSY is estimated 
to be 905 t, with a Bayesian credible interval of between 
740 and 1 055 t. The average biomass estimated for the 
period 1985–1986 is fourfold higher than that estimated for 
the period 2008–2009.
The Fox dynamic model including upwelling effect shows 
a slight improvement of the quality of fit/complexity compro-
mise (the DIC ranges from í19.8 to í20.5 in the case of the 
model with the environmental effect; Table 3). The MSY 
estimated with this model is 935 t for average environ-
mental conditions, varying between 779 and 1 071 t 
depending on the value of the upwelling index. The average 
carrying capacity is estimated to be 23 320 t (Table 3), but 
varies over the period, with values ranging between 16 500 
and 30 000 t (Figure 7).
Cohort and yield-per-recruit analyses
Catches-at-age from age 2 to 14+ were estimated from the 
analysis of the polymodal distribution of the size frequen-
cies (Figure 8a). The corrected pseudo-cohort model shows

that the estimations of fishing mortality are higher than those 
obtained in the case of a usual VPA that does not include 
changes in fishing effort (Figure 8b). The fishery targets 
mainly fish of 4–10 years old, with a maximum mortality of 
0.8 y–1 at age 8 (Figure 8b). Fish older than 10 years are 
rare and less targeted because they concentrate around 
rocky habitats and are therefore less accessible (Domain 
1980). With a lifespan of over 15 years, this species is 
currently exploited at relatively small sizes. 
The yield-per-recruit diagnosis is not very sensitive 
to the hypothesis used for parameter D, which describes 
the increase in the fishing power of the small-scale fleet 
(Figure 8c), or to M (Figure 8d). The analysis indicates 
overexploitation with current fishing effort exceeding the 
effort at the maximum yield-per-recruit by 30% (mEmax   
0.7; Figure 8e). This diagnosis is close to that obtained 
with the biomass dynamic production model including an 
environmental effect (Figure 8e). The biomass per recruit 
model indicates a 77% decrease in abundance, compared 
to the virgin state (Figure 8f). This decrease is also very 
consistent with that estimated from the biomass production 
models.

Discussion and conclusion
One of the main benefits of the approach presented here 
is to show how analysis of various data sources and the 
combination of several methods can reinforce a stock 
assessment analysis, even in the context of poor data 
availability. This multi-pronged approach allows the use 
of various independent sources of data. In particular, 
data obtained through scientific surveys were analysed 
together with commercial CPUE data. The advantage 
of data from surveys is that they come from an optimised 
and standardised sampling protocol. However, they suffer 
from a low sampling rate due to the high cost of sampling. 
Conversely, CPUE from commercial fisheries leads to 
a lower statistical variance of the estimators because of 
the high number of fishery operations that are taken into 
account. However, these data could be biased because 
of the non-proportionality between CPUE data and true 
abundance (unless the hypothesis of a random distribution 
of the fishing effort is assumed; Hilborn and Walters 1992). 
The change in CPUE throughout the years can then be 
linked to changes in fishery strategies (e.g. in fishing areas) 
and, more generally, to changes in fishing power. Hence, 
there are benefits to both correcting these indices using 
an empirical model for the potential trend occurring in the 
fishing power (Gascuel et al. 1993, Gascuel and Ménard 
1997) and combining abundance indices (scientific surveys 
and CPUE from commercial fishery). We can therefore 
check the consistency between estimates from two totally 
independent data sources. For the current case study, 
the index estimated from scientific surveys was highly 
consistent with the index from commercial CPUE when 
the latter index is corrected based on an annual 5% trend 
in the fishing power. A compromise index was constructed 
simply by using the standardised average between the 
two time-series. More sophisticated statistical methods 
(Conn 2010) could be used that are especially useful when 
tendencies between distinct indices do not clearly match.
The use of biomass production models that integrate 
non-stationarity of ecosystem or stock productivity as 
a function of the annual intensity of the upwelling index 
is an important finding in our study. Integration of the 
environmental effect explains a substantial part of the 
model’s residual variability. The correlation between the 
residuals of the Fox model (fitted using a pseudo-equilibrium 
assumption) and the upwelling index wt–n for a time lag (n) 
of 2 years was positive (r2   0.54), thus indicating a positive 
effect of the intensity of the upwelling on the abundance 
of E. aeneus. In particular, the decline observed in the 
abundance indices beginning in 1995 concurs with that 
observed for the upwelling index. This observation is 
reinforced by Santos et al. (2005), who indicated a shift 
in the upwelling regime in the area beginning in 1995. 
Regarding the dynamic model, the effect of the upwelling 
index is consistent with results found in the pseudo-
equilibrium method. Indeed, integrating the environmental 
effect leads to a better adjustment according to the DIC. 
Several hypotheses can be considered for the ecologi cal 
role of the upwelling index. Epinephelus aeneus is found 
along the entire Mauritanian coast, and is characterised by 
seasonal latitudinal migrations, specifically among adults 
(Domain 1980, Bruslé 1985). These migrations can vary 
interannually depending on the intensity of upwelling, which 
triggers migration towards Mauritanian waters, beginning in 
April–July (Cury and Worms 1982, Laurans 2005, Telmidi 
2005). Furthermore, high primary productivities associ-
ated with intense upwelling can increase abundance by 
promoting recruitment (Laurans et al. 2003). The role 
of upwelling in the bottom-up control of marine ecosys-
tems is also well established (Cury and Roy 1989, Bakun 
1990, Cushing 1990). Previous studies have, for instance, 
demonstrated the effects of sea temperature and retention 
processes on recruitment fluctuations for cephalopods in 
the Saharan Bank (North-West Africa) (Demarcq and Faure 
2000, Balguerías et al. 2002). 
The dynamic biomass production model includes 
more parameters than the model fitted under the pseudo-
equilibrium assumption (4 and 2 respectively; see equa -
tions 18 and 16). In addition, some parameters are 
correlated (especially q and K), resulting in a risk of over-
parameterisation and in high uncertainty in the parameter 
estimates and model forecasts (Gascuel and Ménard 1997, 
Gascuel 2008). Thus, the dynamic production model was 
fitted assuming a known ratio between the initial biomass 
B0 and the carrying capacity K and using a Bayesian 
framework to ensure the identifiability of parameters based 
on informative priors. The Bayesian framework also offers 
a flexible modelling framework to account for uncertainties 
related to parameter estimates and the associated 
analyses. Two types of uncertainties, process error and 
observation error, can explain the disparities between 
the observed values and the predicted values. Numerous 
studies have shown that in the case of dynamic models, 
the simultaneous use of these two uncertainty sources in 
the theoretical case of stochastic models with latent states 
(state–space models) generates estimations that are, on 
average, less biased (e.g. Punt and Hilborn 1997, McAllister 
and Kirkwood 1999, Millar and Meyer 2000, Punt 2003, Ono 
et al. 2012). 
In the present case, both models (dynamic or based on a 
pseudo-equilibrium assumption) led to similar results. Nat -
urally, the dynamic model has the advantage of relaxing 
the hypothesis of pseudo-equilibrium, which is constraining 
and often criticised (Hilborn and Walters 1992). At the same 
time, however, the model at equilibrium is generally more 
robust than the dynamic model (Gascuel and Ménard 1997, 
Chassot et al. 2010). It is therefore more precautionary 
to use the two approaches in parallel and to appraise their 
consistency (Fonteneau et al. 1998, Gascuel 2008).
Globally, the diagnoses confirm the excess fishing effort on 
E. aeneus, with values ranging from 30% to 50% compared 
to the current effort (Figure 9). This range is in agreement 
with previous estimates for this species in Senegal, where 
the excess has been estimated by Laurans (2005) to vary 
from 35% to 55%. All methods also converge to show that 
this speciesތ abundance has been severely depleted in 
Mauritania, with a decrease in biomass estimated at around 
80%, compared to the virgin state (Figure 9).
The corrected pseudo-cohorts analysis leads to the same 
consistent diagnosis of overexploitation. This corrected 
analysis is a poorly known method and is only used by a 
few fisheries scientists. Nevertheless, it is a very useful 
Downloaded by [197.231.27.88] at 02:53 14 August 2013 


African Journal of Marine Science 2013, 35(2): 253–267
265
alternative or complement to assess tropical stocks that often 
suffer from a lack of data. This approach was successfully 
used for addressing various temperate fish stocks (Santarelli-
Chaurand 1985, Bertignac 1988) and for marine resources in 
West Africa (Sidibé et al. 2003, Laurans 2005, Chassot et al. 
2008a). In the case of E. aeneus off Mauritania, implemen-
tation of the corrected pseudo-cohort analysis shows that 
when employing two conceptually different assessment 
models (one based on growth and mortality modelling and 
the other on the overall stock production analysis), and 
when using independent data (catches-at-age or historical 
series of catches and abundance indices), we obtained 
results that are close to each other and are globally 
consistent. This convergence of the results evidently 
reinforces the reliability of each model and provides strong 
evidence that the E. aeneus stock of Mauritania is signifi-
cantly overexploited. 
The comparative results of the various analyses, as well as 
an expert judgment based on experience of the assessment, 
leads us to choose a method that can be considered as the 
reference used, for instance, in building fisheries manage-
ment plans. Here, the dynamic model including the environ-
mental effect appeared the most powerful, relaxing the 
pseudo-equilibrium assumption and taking into account the 
former knowledge we have on the impact of upwelling on E. 
aeneus abundance. This model generates results that were 
validated by other models in some ways. More generally, the 
present study shows that by using a multi-method approach 
for stock assessment, we can obtain convergent results and 
a rather precise understanding of the condition of the stock 
of E. aeneus in Mauritania. Cross-validation of the results 
obtained through different methods leads to an assessment 
that is sufficiently robust to be used for definition of concrete 
measures for fishery management.

CHAPITRE 3 : VULNERABILITE DES STOCKS AUX 
EFFETS DE LA PECHE : DIAGNOSTIC DES 
PRINCIPALES RESSOURCES DEMERSALES 
MAURITANIENNES 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


93 
 
Résumé du chapitre 
Le développement récent et rapide de la pêche en Mauritanie offre un cadre 
favorable pour une approche comparative de la résilience des espèces exploitées, 
face à une pression de pêche croissante. Dans un premier temps, on évalue l’état de 
santé d’une sélection de 22 stocks démersaux caractérisés par des écologies et des 
stratégies démographiques contrastées, ainsi que par des états d’exploitation 
différents. Un modèle de production de biomasse dynamique est ajusté dans un 
cadre statistique bayésien aux séries d’indices d’abondance estimées par 
modélisation delta-GLM des données issues des campagnes scientifiques de 
chalutage. On montre que 12 des 22 stocks évalués sont surexploités et 3 
pleinement exploités. Le diagnostic cumulé sur l’ensemble des stocks fait apparaitre 
une situation de surexploitation globale, avec une biomasse divisée par environ 3 
depuis 1982 et un effort de pêche supérieur de 30% à l’effort de maximisation EMSY 
(et 40% pour les seuls poissons). Dans un second temps, les relations existant entre 
état des stocks et traits d’histoire de vie sont analysées. On montre que les stocks 
les plus vulnérables et les stocks d’espèces de grande taille sont ceux qui supportent 
la pression de pêche la plus élevée, mais également ceux qui sont actuellement les 
plus surexploités.  
A l’échelle du peuplement représenté par l’ensemble des stocks considérés les 
indicateurs moyens de vulnérabilité (MV), de taille maximale moyenne (MML) et de 
niveau trophique moyen (MTL) ne présentent pas d’évolution très cohérente. Ceci 
s’explique par des niveaux et des histoires d’exploitation qui diffèrent d’un stock à 
l’autre. A l’inverse, ces mêmes indicateurs exprimés en fonction d’un multiplicateur 
d’effort de pêche ou d’une mortalité par pêche sont fortement décroissants, 
confirmant ainsi que l’intensification de l’exploitation conduit à des peuplements 
dominés par des espèces de plus petite taille et de plus faible niveau trophique 
 
 
 


94 
 
Vulnerability and overfishing of marine resources: some lessons 
1 
from the assessment of demersal stocks off Mauritania 
2 
 
3 
B Meissa1,2* and D Gascuel2 
4 
 
5 
1Institut Mauritanien de Recherches Océanographiques et des Pêches (IMROP), BP: 22, Nouadhibou, 
6 
Mauritania 
7 
2Université Européenne de Bretagne, Agrocampus Ouest, UMR985 Ecologie et santé des 
8 
écosystèmes, 65 rue de Saint Brieuc, CS 84215, 35042 Rennes cedex, France 
9 
* Corresponding author, E-mail: bmouldhabib@gmail.com 
10 
 
11 
3.1. 
Abstract 
12 
 
13 
The recent, rapid development of fishing in Mauritania offers a good case study for a 
14 
comparative approach of the resilience of the species exploited there, in the face of 
15 
increasing fishing pressure. First, we assessed the health of 22 demersal stocks with 
16 
differing ecological requirements, demographic strategies, and states of exploitation. 
17 
A dynamic production model was fitted in a framework of Bayesian statistics to a 
18 
series of abundance indices estimated by delta-GLM modeling of data from scientific 
19 
trawl surveys. Twelve of the 22 stocks assessed are overexploited and three are fully 
20 
exploited. Combined assessment of all 22 stocks shows overall overexploitation, with 
21 
biomass decreasing by about 75% since 1982 and fishing effort 30% higher than that 
22 
at maximum sustained yield (40% higher for finfish). Second, relations between 
23 
states of stocks and life-history traits were analyzed. The most vulnerable stocks and 
24 
stocks of large species withstand the highest fishing pressure and are those that are 
25 
the most overexploited. At the scale of the community represented by the stocks 
26 
considered, indictors of mean vulnerability, mean maximum length, and mean trophic 
27 
level do not change in a consistent manner. This can be explained by different levels 
28 
and histories of exploitation. In contrast, these same indicators expressed as a 
29 
function of a multiplier of fishing effort or fishing mortality decrease strongly, thus 
30 


95 
 
confirming that the intensification of exploitation leads to communities dominated by 
31 
smaller species and lower trophic levels. 
32 
Keywords: Bayesian approach, Demersal resources, Fishing down the marine food 
33 
web, Mauritania, Overfishing, Resilience, Stock assessment, Surplus production 
34 
model, Trophic level, Vulnerability 
35 
 
36 
3.2. Introduction 
37 
 
38 
The vulnerability of exploited marine species depends on the interaction 
39 
between the extrinsic pressure that they are subject to and their intrinsic capacity to 
40 
react to them. In general, large slow-growing species with a later age at maturity are 
41 
more vulnerable to fishing (Gislason 2003, Reynolds et al. 2005). Many empirical 
42 
studies using abundance data of exploited fish populations have shown a significant 
43 
correlation between the rate of decline and life-history traits (Dulvy et al. 2004, 
44 
Jennings et al. 1998, Reynolds et al. 2005). Thus, lacking the data necessary to 
45 
apply conventional methods for estimating stock size of most species of exploited 
46 
fish, researchers have used biological and ecological life-history traits to indirectly 
47 
measure the potential impact of fishing on marine resources and identify the most 
48 
vulnerable species (Cheung et al. 2005 et 2007, Jennings et al., 1998). In particular, 
49 
Cheung et al. (2005) define an index of the vulnerability of marine species to fishing 
50 
effects, estimated from life-history traits and ecological characteristics of these 
51 
species. Most studies in this domain use comparative approaches and meta-
52 
analyses that aim to identify the main eco-biological factors that determine the 
53 
vulnerability of exploited stocks to fishing effects (Myers and Mertz 1998). Using 
54 
hierarchical Bayesian models, Zhou et al. (2012) identified natural mortality as the 
55 
parameter with the highest impact on the biological reference points used to manage 
56 
fishing. Jennings et al. (1998) revealed a significant relation between the maximum 
57 
length of species and their abundance by considering phylogenetic relationships 
58 
between stocks, even if length is strongly influenced by fishing (Daan et al. 2005). 
59 
Based on commercial catches, Pauly et al. (1998) revealed changes in the 
60 
trophic structures of fish communities caused by fishing; they attribute this 
61 
phenomena of “fishing down the marine food web” to a higher vulnerability of high 
62 


96 
 
trophic levels. The role of trophic levels in the resilience of exploited marine 
63 
populations has since become the subject of numerous debates. Use of the mean 
64 
trophic level of catches as an indicator of exploitation, in particular, has been 
65 
questioned by many studies. Essington et al. (2006) show that changes in fishing 
66 
strategy may explain the decrease in mean trophic level of catches without observing 
67 
an equivalent decrease in the ecosystem itself; this is called “fishing through the 
68 
marine food web”. More generally, Branch et al. (2010) disagree that these changes 
69 
occur globally, thus questioning whether species of high trophic level are the most 
70 
vulnerable. Many studies based on data from scientific surveys, however, have 
71 
shown that changes in trophic structure affect not only catches but also the 
72 
underlying ecosystem, thus confirming the existence of “fishing down the marine food 
73 
web”. In western Africa, a significant decrease in the biomass of high trophic levels 
74 
and a relative increase in low trophic levels have been observed off the coasts of 
75 
Senegal and Guinea (Laurans et al. 2004) and Mauritania (Gascuel et al. 2007a). 
76 
Western Africa, particularly Mauritania, is a good case study for studying the 
77 
impact of fishing on resources and ecosystems, identifying factors influencing 
78 
species resilience, and analyzing larger ecosystem changes such as “fishing down 
79 
the marine food web”. This region is experiencing rapid development of fishing. In 
80 
Mauritania, the main fishing resources, notably coastal demersal fish, were little 
81 
exploited until the end of the 1960s (Josse et Garcia 1986, Garibaldi and Grainger 
82 
2004, Gascuel et al. 2007b). Fishing effort has greatly increased during the last few 
83 
decades (Chavance 2004), which has led to a severe decrease in biomass and 
84 
serious signs of overexploitation of demersal resources (Christensen et al. 2005, 
85 
Gascuel et al. 2007a, Meissa et al. 2013). There is thus a strong contrast in the 
86 
series of data available. Naturally, not all species are affected in the same way or 
87 
have the same response to increasing fishing pressure (Labrosse et al. 2010) and 
88 
thus, there is a contrast among species. However, the situation of most stocks 
89 
remains poorly known, stock assessments until now having focused only on small 
90 
pelagic species and a small number of demersal species from targeted catches. 
91 
This study thus aims to quantify the impact of fishing on the demersal resources 
92 
of Mauritania and analyze the factors that influence the resilience of stocks to fishing 
93 
effects, via a comparative-ecology approach applied to 22 taxa. First, the response of 
94 
the stocks to fishing pressure, which has strongly increased over the past two or 
95 


97 
 
three decades, is analyzed. Abundance indices are estimated via delta-Generalized 
96 
Linear Model (GLM) modeling of data from scientific trawl surveys performed on the 
97 
Mauritian continental shelf since 1982. A dynamic production model was calibrated to 
98 
these data in a Bayesian statistics framework; it reveals the impact of fishing of stock 
99 
abundance. Second, we sought to identify the factors that explain the variability in 
100 
resilience of stocks (demographics, vulnerability index, trophic level, habitat, etc.). 
101 
The parameters of the response function of the stocks to the increasing fishing 
102 
pressure were thus compared to eco-biological parameters using several analysis 
103 
tools: multivariate analyses, statistical tests of relations between indicators of stock 
104 
state and eco-biological parameters, and mean change in the indicators at the scale 
105 
of the community considered. 
106 
 
107 
3.3. Material and Methods 
108 
 
109 
3.3.1. Species selection and data used 
110 
 
111 
The species included were selected according to two criteria: their commercial 
112 
importance and the availability of reliable information about their abundance and 
113 
catches, in particular their taxonomic identification. The choice of species was then 
114 
refined to cover as much as possible the large diversity of their demographic 
115 
strategies and eco-biological characteristics. For three genera, determination down to 
116 
species level is uncertain in catch data, as in survey data; so, we preferred to 
117 
analyze them at the genus level. These three were tonguesole (Cynoglossus spp., 
118 
which groups mainly C. canariensis and C. senegalensis), croakers (Pseudotolithus 
119 
senegalensis and P.typus) and sea catfish (Carlarius heudolotii and Arius spp. such 
120 
as A. parkii). In all, 22 demersal taxa were selected, divided into three groups: fish, 
121 
cephalopods, and crustaceans. These taxa represent approximately 60% of the 
122 
demersal catches currently made in Mauritania. 
123 
Three databases managed by the Mauritanian Institute of Oceanographic and 
124 
Fisheries Research (IMROP) were used: 
125 


98 
 
• All scientific demersal surveys performed along the Mauritanian continental 
126 
shelf from 1982-2010, totaling 5,532 trawls in 65 surveys. Each observation 
127 
corresponds to one trawl for a standard duration of 30 minutes. Since fishing 
128 
gear was modified in 1989, correction coefficients estimated by Gascuel et al. 
129 
(2007a) were applied to data gathered prior to that date. 
130 
• Logbooks of national and foreign industrial fishing since 1990, which provide 
131 
catch data aggregated by commercial categories. These data were 
132 
disaggregated into species using profiles of species composition by license 
133 
type estimated from data from the fisheries observation program. 
134 
• A small-scale fishing database covering 1994-2010. Catches from 1990-1993 
135 
were estimated from the number of dugouts per year and the mean catch per 
136 
pirogue from 1994-1996 (Gascuel et al. 2007b). Given the small number of 
137 
dugouts during this period, related catches are relatively low.  
138 
Catches before 1990 are available in the scientific literature for two species: octopus 
139 
(Octopus vulgaris) since 1982 and white grouper (Epinephelus aeneus) since 1985. 
140 
 
141 
3.3.2. Abundance index 
142 
 
143 
Annual abundance indices were estimated by linear modeling of scientific 
144 
survey data for all taxa regularly fished during these surveys. Due to the large 
145 
number of null values (ca. 40-90%), a delta-GLM zero-inflated model was used 
146 
(Stefansson 1996, Meissa et al. 2008 and 2013). This zero-inflated model combines 
147 
the following two submodels:  
148 
• A presence-absence sub-model, which estimates the probability that each 
149 
taxon will be present during each trawl. The GLM based on assuming a binomial 
150 
distribution is expressed as follows:

169

170 
et al. 2013). Two types of abundance indices were used: catch per unit effort (CPUE) 
171 
of the small-scale fishery targeting fish in the Banc d’Arguin zone, for meagre 
172 
(Argyrosomus regius) and two species of mullet (Mugil capurrii and M. cephalus)

fishery for two shrimp species (Parapenaeus longirostris and Farfantepaeus notialis)

and European squid (Loligo vulgaris). 
176 
 
177 
3.3.3. Stock-assessment model 
178 
 
179 
Stock assessments were performed by adjusting a biomass-production model 
180 
(Fox 1970) in a Bayesian framework (McAllister and Kirkwood 1998, Meissa et al. 
181 
2013, Ono et al. 2012). The Bayesian stochastic process calculates the biomass of 
182 


100 
 
year t+1 from that of year t, with a process error around the biomass-production 
183 
function, estimated as:

191
